Overall retaliation (M = 7.48, SD = 1.48, CI [7.24, 7.72]) was judged to be far more predictive of loyalty than non-retaliation (M = 4.65, SD = 2.00, CI [4.33, 4.97]; t(275.93) = 13.92, p < .001, d = 1.61, CI [1.34, 1.87]). People rated the actions of the retaliator acting for himself as least indicative of characteristic loyalty (M = 6.71, SD = 1.57, CI [6.26, 7.16]) and the friend's actions most indicative (M = 8.27, SD = 0.96, CI [8.00, 8.54]); the two conditions differed significantly (t(79.01) = 5.97, p < .001, d = 1.20, CI [0.76, 1.63]). Participants also considered the actions of the retaliator acting on his friend's behalf to be more diagnostic of general loyalty than those of the third-party retaliator (M = 7.42, SD = 1.43, CI [7.00, 7.83]; t(81.70) = 3.49, p < .001, d = 0.71, CI [0.29, 1.11]). In addition, there was a significant difference between the self and third-party conditions (t(94.50) = 2.31, p = .023, d = 0.47, CI [0.06, 0.87]).

On the non-retaliation side, the agent who failed to act for himself was rated most loyal (M = 5.41, SD = 2.07, CI [4.81, 6.00]), which was significantly higher than the friend who was deemed most characteristically disloyal (M = 4.10, SD = 1.76, CI [3.60, 4.61]; t(93.55) = 3.37, p = .001, d = 0.68, CI [0.27, 1.09]). Pairwise comparisons also revealed that the self condition significantly differed from the third-party condition (M = 4.45, SD = 1.98, CI [3.91, 5.00]; t(98.44) = 2.38, p = .019, d = 0.47, CI [0.08, 0.87]); the other two conditions did not differ from one another (t(99.86) = 0.95, p = .345, d = 0.19, CI [-0.20, 0.58]).

Enhanced Model: Loyalty as an Additional Predictor in Praiseworthiness ANOVA

Adding loyalty-and centering it for interaction terms-substantially improved model fit (adjusted R² = .36 → .57; ΔR²adj = .213). Fit indices also supported this interpretation (baseline without loyalty: Akaike Information Criterion (AIC) = 1194.18; Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC) = 1220.09; enhanced model: AIC = 1079.13, BIC = 1127.23; ΔAIC = 115.06, ΔBIC = 92.85), as confirmed by direct comparison: F(6, 287) = 25.33, p < .001. Notably, there was a strong main effect of loyalty (b = 0.63, SE = 0.06; F(1, 287) = 120.63, p < .001, p2 = .30), along with a significant three-way interaction with agent and action (F(2, 287) = 3.97, p = .020, p2 = .03). (See Tables 2 (main model; compact) vs. 3 (loyalty predictor and interactions). Adding loyalty also attenuated the effect of action and rendered agent × action non-significant. A main effect of agent also emerged, which was not present in the main, compact model. These results collectively suggest that loyalty is both a powerful predictor and moderator of praiseworthiness evaluations. (Mediation results-agent × action → loyalty (centered)→ praiseworthiness-are provided in the Supplemental Materials.)

Additional Measures: Principal-Component Analyses

Morality, altruism, and heroism all correlated closely with praiseworthiness and loyalty though, they seemed, once again, to coincide most closely on the non-retaliation side.

Importantly, the correlations for specific conditions between loyalty and praiseworthiness were consistent with one another: One might suppose that loyalty for a friend would be more correlated with praiseworthiness than other conditions, but this was not the case. (Additional statistics are available in Supplemental Materials.) Here we describe and provide the results for the principal-component analysis (PCA). We first standardized (z-scored) all five measures-praiseworthiness, loyalty, morality, altruism, and heroism-and combined them into a PCA that yielded a dominant Principal Component 1 (PC1), accounting for 78.87%. PC2 accounted for an additional 7% of the variance (see Figure 2 above). Further, all five measures loaded closely in magnitude (.43-.47) and direction, suggesting that PC1 represents prosociality, as the measures-with the possible exception of praiseworthiness-all can be construed as specifically relating to socially beneficial considerations. We, therefore, used only PC1 as a DV to conduct a 3 (agent) × 2 (action) fullfactorial ANOVA. The overall model was significant: F(5, 293) = 61.91, p < .001, with an R² adj = .51.

Results failed to produce a main effect of agent (F(2, 293) = 0.33, p = .721, p2 = .00), though there was a main effect of action (F(1, 293) = 277.61, p < .001, p2 = .49). The interaction between agent and action was also significant (F(2, 293) = 14.86, p < .001, p2 = .09). Levene's test (F(5, 293) = 1.22, p = .298) provided support for the use of independent-samples comparisons. PC1 as Dependent Variable Note. Means ± SEs from a 3 (agent: self, friend, third party) × 2 (action: retaliation, nonretaliation) ANOVA with Principal Component 1 (PC1) scores as the dependent measure.

Comparisons provided support for the Prosociality and Integrity Hypotheses, respectively. As expected, retaliation (M = 1.36, SD = 1.25, CI [1.16, 1.57]) and no-retaliation strongly differed (M = -1.34, SD = 1.63, CI [-1.60, -1.07]; t(297) = 16.02, p < .001, d = 1.85, CI [1.58, 2.12]). On the retaliation side, acting for a friend (M = 1.75, SD = 1.24, CI [1.40, 2.10]) was substantially different than acting for oneself (M = 0.84, SD = 1.18, CI [0.50, 1.18]; t(98) = 3.77, p < .001, d = 0.75, CI [0.35, 1.16]), as was acting for a third-party stranger (M = 1.49, SD = 1.17, CI [1.15, 1.83]); t(95.0) = 2.71, p = .008, d = 0.55, CI [0.14, 0.95]. Friend and third party did not differ: t(97) = 1.08, p = .283, d = 0.22, CI [-0.18, 0.61].

The non-retaliation side was a near mirror image of the retaliation side with failing to act on one's own behalf (M = -0.68, SD = 1.82, CI [-1.20, -0.16]), differing from choosing not to retaliate for both friend (M = -1.90, SD = 1.20, CI [-2.25, -1.56]; t(96) = 3.92, p < .001, d = 0.79, CI [0.38, 1.20]) and third party (M = -1.42, SD = 1.61, CI [-1.86, -0.98]; t(100) = 2.17, p = .032, d = 0.43, CI [0.04, 0.82]) The friend and third-party conditions were not significantly different (t(100) = 1.71, p = .091, d = 0.34, CI [-0.05, 0.73]). Notably, acting for a friend was greatest in magnitude and lowest in magnitude for retaliation and non-retaliation, respectively.

Study 1 Discussion

The dramatic nature of the scenario may account for the lack of differentiation among the retaliators. Indeed, this scenario may have qualified less as "retaliation" than as a justice scenario-retrieving a stolen item-with the "shouting" component being too understated to register clearly as retaliation. Whether or not it constituted unequivocal retaliation, it was extremely well-received.

In accordance with the Prosociality Hypothesis, retaliating for oneself was rated least praiseworthy; as predicted by the Integrity Hypothesis, abstaining from retaliation for oneself rated significantly higher than the friend condition. In the PCA analyses, retaliation for oneself was significantly less positive than its counterpart conditions and choosing not to retaliate for oneself was more positive. Retaliating for a friend was rated highest, failing to retaliate for a friend lowest.

Such results add conceptual complexity, especially considering that (1) Participants evaluated retaliation and abstaining partially independently; (2) failing to act for a friend was rated as especially blameworthy (followed by failing a third-party); (3) loyalty exhibited strong main, moderating, and mediating effects. These findings also argue for one possibility discussed in our Hypothesis 2: that retaliation may be the particular domain of allies.

STUDY 2: Study 2a) Overt, Office-Confrontation Scenarios vs. Study 2b) Covert, "Office-Promotion,"

STUDY 2: Study 2a) Overt, Office-Confrontation Scenarios vs. Study 2b) Covert, "Office-Promotion,"

Scenarios

Study 2 had several objectives: First, to examine retaliation in isolation; second, to investigate the implications of using two scenarios that are clearly less criminal (relative to the theft) and situated in a more naturalistic, well-established context-an office setting.

foot_0 Finally, we investigated the effects of proportionality and transparency by comparing an "overt" (Study 2a) scenario and a "covert" scenario (Study 2b).

Methods

Participants

A total of 301 participants (M(age)= 39.0, SD = 11.9) were recruited through Turk Prime for the Studies 2a and 2b, completing the experiment in a median time of 4.72 minutes. 142 were female. Once again, we had aimed for 300 to produce 50/cell, but due to slight drop-out, the cells were not precisely even (see below). Participants were compensated $0.40. None were excluded from analyses. Study 2a and Study 2b employed the same participants.

Design and Procedure

As in Study 1, we used a 3 (Agent: self vs. friend vs. third party) × 2 (Action: retaliation vs. no retaliation) design. However, in this mixed design, each participant read and responded to two vignettes (in counterbalanced order): one "overt" (Study 2a) and one "covert" (Study 2b).

Participants were thus randomly assigned to two of the twelve total conditions, with half reading and completing the overt scenario first and the other half first assigned to the covert scenario.

In each vignette, the agent chose either to retaliate or refrain from doing so on behalf of themselves, a friend, or a relatively unknown coworker-this time in an office setting, where retaliation is known to occur (Bordia et al., 2014; Tripp & Bies, 2009). After each vignette, participants responded to six 9-point Likert scale items, including praiseworthiness and loyalty (our primary DVs). To bolster these measures, we also included two additional items: how "good or bad" the agent's actions were and how "good or bad a friend" they were perceived to be.

These were highly correlated with praiseworthiness and loyalty, respectively. The full procedure was then repeated with the second vignette.

The Scenarios (Tables 5 and 6). In constructing these two vignettes, we sought to: (1) assess whether results would replicate or diverge from Study 1; and (2) examine reactions to retaliation differing in transparency and proportionality. In the "overt" scenario (Study 2a), retaliation involved a direct, public confrontation of the offender (i.e., transparent), designed to be congruent with the original offense (i.e., proportional)-or warranted. In contrast, in the "covert" vignette (Study 2b), retaliation took the form of a private, negative, and unrelated report to a supervisor, without the offender-or any onlookers-present. We anticipated that the overt response would be more readily perceived as warranted and that the covert response would elicit more misgivings and/or ambivalence.

We were especially interested in testing two predictions derived from our hypotheses.

First, we expected that in the overt scenario, retaliation for a friend would be rated most positively-a prediction that was largely supported by Study 1 findings. Second, we hypothesized that in the covert scenario, choosing not to retaliate on one's own behalf might be evaluated more positively than retaliating for oneself, potentially reflecting restraint or integrity when retaliation is not clearly deemed justified.

Because of a minor typographical error, self conditions for Study 2a were run again; results did not materially differ. (See Supplemental Materials for further explanation.)

Table 5

Scenario for Study 2a: Set-up for the Vignettes in Each of the Six "Overt" Conditions.

Condition Text

Set-up (varying by condition) SELF: Jamie and Nick are long-time co-workers at a company; the two don't know each other very well but have been recently assigned to jointly lead a very lucrative project for an important client. The project involves a large team and much creative and arduous work.

Technically having the higher job title, Jamie has the authority to oversee the team and remove people at his discretion. At an early meeting, Nick shocks Jamie by taking credit for a lot of his work, mocking him each time he tries to state an opinion, and basically dictating the next steps that he thinks him should be taking in the project.

FRIEND: Jamie and Casey are long-time co-workers at a company, who have become good friends over the years and mutually relied upon one another's help and support. They also work with Nick, though neither of them knows him very well. The three have recently been assigned to jointly lead a very lucrative project for an important client. The project involves a large team and much creative and arduous work. Technically having the highest job title of the three, Jamie has the authority to oversee the team and remove people at this discretion. At an early meeting, Nick shocks Jamie by taking credit for a lot of Casey's work, mocking Casey each time he tries to state an opinion, and basically dictating the next steps that he thinks Casey should be taking in the project.

THIRD PARTY: Jamie, Casey and Nick are long-time co-workers at a company. The three don't know each other very well but recently have been assigned to jointly lead a group project for an important client. The project involves a large team and much creative and arduous work. Technically having the highest job title of the three, Jamie has the authority to oversee the team and remove people at his discretion. At an early meeting, Nick shocks Jamie by taking credit for a lot of Casey's work, mocking Casey each time he tries to state an opinion and basically dictating the next steps he thinks that Casey should be taking on the project.

Retaliation [Agent: Jamie] is mortified by Nick's behavior and knows it to be wrong. In front of the whole group, he sternly reprimands Nick and throws him off of the project. Non-retaliation [Agent: Jamie] is mortified by Nick's behavior and knows it to be wrong yet decides that it's not worth confronting Nick. He doesn't say anything in response.

Note. As in Study 1, "self" is in red, "friend" in blue, and "third party" in green; the offender appears in bold font. The same applies to the scenario in Table 6 (Study 2b: Covert).

As described above, participants were then prompted to answer questions on 9-point Likert scales. The praiseworthiness and loyalty, along with the two additional "goodness" and "friendship" questions, were counterbalanced. They then proceeded to read one of six versions of the "covert" vignette, printed below in Table 6, answering the same questions. Approximately half of participants read the covert scenario first.

Retaliation Condition

While in his boss's office, the subject of the promotion comes up.

[Agent: John] believes that Adam and the other candidate-who currently have the same title and responsibilities-have similar qualifications; however, he tells his boss that he thinks that the other candidate is more deserving and that his boss should choose him over Adam for the position.

Non-retaliation Condition

While in his boss's office, the subject of the promotion comes up.

[Agent: John] believes that Adam and the other candidate-who currently have the same title and responsibilities-have similar qualifications and tells his boss so. He says he can't reasonably assert that one deserves the position over the other.

Study 2a (Overt Scenario) Results

Overt Positivity: Praiseworthiness / Goodness-of-Action Composite

The praiseworthiness and goodness-of-action measures were highly correlated (r = .88, CI [.85, .90], p < .001), and we, therefore, proceeded to combine the DVs for the remainder of the positivity analyses. As depicted in Figure 4, praiseworthiness, graphed alone, and praiseworthiness combined with the goodness-of-action measures produced essentially identical results. (See Table 7 for all Study-2 model results.) Overt-Results for Praiseworthiness and Positivity Note. Study 2a, overt scenario: (A) Evaluations of praiseworthiness alone, and (B) composite evaluations of praiseworthiness and the goodness-of-action measure ("positivity") as a function of agent identity (self, friend, third party) and action (retaliation, no retaliation). Error bars represent SEs of the means. Cell sizes were as follows: for the retaliation conditions-self with typo (n = 50), corrected self (n = 49), friend (n = 50), third party (n = 50); for the no-retaliation conditions-self with typo (n = 46), corrected self (n = 49), friend (n = 52), third party (n = 53).

Corrected self values are in dark red; lighter red bars reflect original (typo) values. Overarching brackets reflect the main effect of action.

The overall model was significant: F(5, 297) = 48.98, p < .001, with an R² adj = .44. The 3 × 2 factorial ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of action (F(1, 297) = 217.24, p < .001, p2 = .42), though only a marginal main effect of agent (F(2, 297) = 2.68, p = .070, p2 = .02).

The interaction between the agent and action variables was significant (F(2, 297) = 9.50, p < .001, p2 = .06), further justifying our planned, follow-up comparisons. The model met the assumption of the homogeneity of variance (Levene's test: F(5, 297) = 1.09, p = .37).

Consistent with the main effect and collapsing across agent, overall retaliation (M = 6.88, SD = 1.88, CI [6.58, 7.18]), was considered much more positive than choosing not to retaliate (M = 3.72, SD = 1.95, CI [3.41, 4.03]; t(301) = 14.37, p < .001, d = 1.65, CI [1.39, 1.91]).

Expanding upon the agent × action interaction, retaliation on behalf of a friend (M = 7.46, SD = 1.66, CI [6.99, 7.93]) was deemed significantly higher than both retaliating for oneself (M = 6.50, SD = 1.67, CI [6.02, 6.98]; t(97) = 2.87, p = .005, d = 0.58, CI [0.17, 0.98]) and for a third party (M = 6.67, SD = 2.16, CI [6.06, 7.28]; t(98) = 2.05, p = .043, d = 0.41, CI [0.01, 0.81]).

The third-party retaliator's ratings did not differ from those of the retaliator who acted for himself (t(97) = 0.44, p = .66, d = 0.09, CI [-0.31, 0.48]).

On the non-retaliation side, the actions of the agent who chose to abstain on his own behalf (M = 4.61, SD = 1.82, CI [4.09, 5.14]) were considered less positive than his nonretaliating counterparts in the friend (M = 3.33, SD = 1.86, CI [2.81, 3.85]; t(99) = 3.50, p < .001, d = 0.70, CI [0.29, 1.10]) and third party conditions (M = 3.27, SD = 1.90, CI [2.75, 3.80]; t(100) = 3.63, p < .001, d = 0.72, CI [0.32, 1.12]). The friend and third-party conditions were almost even: t(103) = 0.145, p = .885 (as reflected by a d that was essentially 0, d = 0.03, CI [-0.35, 0.41]) -unlike in Study 1's park scenario in which third party was rated higher than friend. Overt-Loyalty Evaluations Note. Study 2a: Evaluations of loyalty/good-friend combined measures as a function of agent identity (self, friend, third party) and action (retaliation, no retaliation). Bars represent SEs. The ns for each cell are the same as in the previous DV.

Overt: Loyalty / Likelihood-of-Being-a-Good-Friend Composite

The loyalty and friend ratings were likewise highly correlated (Pearson's r = 0.79, CI [0.74, 0.83], p < .001). Accordingly, they were combined into a single loyalty composite (LF_composite) for the following analyses. In addition, the corrected self conditions (re-run) did not differ from the original conditions that included the typo (see Supplemental Materials).

The adjusted coefficient of determination for the following model was R² adj = .45 (F(5, 297) = 49.63, p < .001). A 3 × 2 factorial ANOVA produced a significant main effect of agent (F(2, 297) = 5.26, p = .006, p2 = .03), a main effect of action (F(1, 297) = 145.88, p < .001, p2 = .33), as well as a robust interaction between the two (F(2, 297) = 43.06, p < .001, p2 = .22).

The model met the assumption of variance homogeneity (Levene's test: F(5, 297) = 1.28, p = .27).

The retaliation results produced a similar pattern of results to those of the praiseworthiness and positivity, respectively. Overall retaliation (M = 6.85, SD = 1.73, CI [6.57, 7.13]) was greater than overall non-retaliation (M = 4.58, SD = 1.93, CI [4.27, 4.89]; t(301) = 10.75, p < .001, d = 1.24, CI [0.99, 1.48]). The retaliator acting for a friend (M = 7.71, SD = 1.41, CI [7.31, 8.11]) was evaluated as much more globally loyal and likely to be a good friend than both the retaliator acting for himself (M = 6.01, SD = 1.47, CI [5.59, 6.43]; t(97) = 5.89, p < .001, d = 1.18, CI [0.75, 1.61]) and the retaliator acting for a third party (M = 6.81, SD = 1.87, CI [6.28, 7.34]; t(98) = 2.71, p = .008, d = 0.54, CI [0.14, 0.94]). The third-party retaliator was also deemed significantly more characteristically loyal than the retaliator acting upon his own behalf: t(97) = 2.36, p = .020, d = 0.47, CI [0.07, 0.87].

Within non-retaliation, the retaliator acting for himself (M = 6.15, SD = 1.61, CI [5.69, 6.62]) was considered more likely to be loyal than either of the other two conditions, friend (M = 3.80, SD = 1.65, CI [3.34, 4.26]; t(99) = 7.24, p < .001, d = 1.44, CI [1.00, 1.88]) and third party (M = 3.89, SD = 1.58, CI [3.45, 4.32]; t(100) = 7.16, p < .001, d = 1.42, CI [0.98, 1.85]). As with praiseworthiness, the third-party and friend conditions were almost even: t(103) = 0.28, p = .78, d = 0.05, CI [-0.33, 0.44].

Overt: Enhanced Model: Loyalty Composite Added as an Additional Predictor in Positivity ANOVA

We ran a full-factorial ANOVA that included the loyalty composite as a predictor and moderator (through interactions with agent and action). Recall that the coefficient of determination for the main, compact model (agent × action only) was R² adj = .44. Adding the loyalty composite (grand-mean centered) significantly improved the model's explanatory power (R² adj (enhanced) = .68, ΔR 2 adj = .22); comparing the two models produced a significant improvement in fit (F(6, 291) = 37.18, p < .001), with ΔAIC = 160.41, ΔBIC = 138.13. This enhanced model thus yielded a robust overall fit (F(11, 291) = 58.81, p < .001). The loyalty composite revealed a strong main effect (b = 0.71, SE = 0.05; F(1, 291) = 189.91, p < .001, p2 = .39) and interacted with agent (F(2, 291) = 7.00, p = .001, p2 = .05). Importantly, once loyalty was introduced, the previously significant agent × action interaction was reduced to marginal significance (p2 = .02, p = .057). Full results appear in

Table 7. (As with Study 1, mediation results-agent × action → loyalty composite → positivity-are in Supplemental Materials.) counterbalanced (half read the covert vignette first, half the overt). As discussed above, overt retaliation may be judged differently than covert: (1) Retaliation itself may be considered less commendable; and (2) if retaliation is less commendable, patterns among agents may shift. For example, retaliation for a friend may cease to be most praiseworthy and abstaining for oneself may be evaluated more positively. We set out to test these predictions within the framework of our broader hypotheses.

Study 2b (Covert Scenario) Results

Covert Positivity: Praiseworthiness & Goodness-of-Action Composite

Overall, the praiseworthiness and goodness-of-action ratings were highly correlated overall at Pearson's r = .81, CI [.77, .85], p < .001, though the strength of this association varied somewhat across conditions (see brief discussion of self: retaliation vs. no retaliation below).

Accordingly, we combined them into a single positivity index. Covert-Results for Praiseworthiness and Positivity, Respectively Note. Study 2b-Evaluations of (A) Praiseworthiness and (B) Composite positivity as discussed, the "self" conditions were re-run in the overt-scenario analyses.

The overall model was significant: F(5, 295) = 4.19, p < .001, with an R² adj = .05, notably accounting for much less variance than in previous studies. A 3 × 2 factorial ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of agent (F(2, 295) = 4.46, p = .012, p2 = .03), though the main effect of action (F(1, 295) = 1.65, p = .200, p2 = .00) was not significant. Nonetheless, the interaction between agent and action was significant (F(2, 295) = 5.15, p = .006, p2 = .03). Levene's test (F(5, 295) = .53, p = .754) supported the assumption of homogeneity.

As one clear departure from Studies 1 and 2a, overall retaliation (M = 5.65, SD = 1.70, CI [5.37, 5.92]) and non-retaliation (M = 5.40, SD = 1.90, CI [5.10, 5.70]) did not differ (t(299) = 1.19, p = .236, d = 0.14, CI [-0.09, 0.36]) and were each only narrowly above the midpoint, (5). With regard to retaliation, Study 2b yielded the same pattern as its predecessor studies, but no comparisons were significant. The retaliator acting for a friend (M = 5.94, SD = 1.81; CI [5.40, 6.47]) was rated higher than either of the other two conditions, but neither comparison reached significance, neither with self (M = 5.46, SD = 1.57, CI [5.01, 5.91]); t(95) = 1.38, p = .170, d = 0.28, CI [-0.12, 0.68]), nor third-party condition (M = 5.57, SD = 1.73, CI [5.08, 6.06]; t(95) = 1.02, p = .311, d = 0.21, CI [-0.19, 0.61]), respectively. Self and third party also did not differ: t(98) = 0.33, p = .740, d = 0.07, CI [-0.33, 0.46].

On the non-retaliation side, abstaining on one's own behalf (M = 6.07, SD = 1.70, CI [5.60, 6.53]) was rated more positively than abstaining for a third party (M = 4.63, SD = 1.97, CI [4.07, 5.18], t(102) = 3.99, p < .001, d = 0.78, CI [0.38, 1.18]) but not more than abstaining for a friend (M = 5.49, SD = 1.75, CI [4.99, 5.99]; t(101) = 1.69, p = .094, d = 0.33, CI [-0.06, 0.72]).

Unlike earlier studies, failing to act for a friend was rated less blameworthy than failing to act for a third party (t(99) = 2.32, p = .022, d = 0.46, CI [0.07, 0.86]), producing a "staircase" pattern.

Finally, in a distinctive deviation from either of the other two studies, participants rated abstaining from retaliation more positively than retaliating for oneself. It's also worth noting that while, for some conditions, praiseworthiness and positivity were almost identical, this was not the case for the "self conditions." In the context of praiseworthiness, alone, refraining from retaliation (M = 6.15 , SD = 1.77, CI [5.70, 6.61]) vs. retaliating for oneself (M = 5.34, SD = 1.70, CI [4.86, 5.82]) were significantly different: t(101) = 2.37, p = .020, d = 0.47, CI [0.07, 0.86]. However, when praiseworthiness was combined with goodness-of-action into the positivity composite, the difference was attenuated (t(101) = 1.88, p = .063, d = 0.37, CI [-0.02, 0.76]). (See Figure 6 above.) Regardless, choosing not to retaliate for oneself received the highest positivity rating of all six conditions.

Covert Loyalty / Likelihood-of-Being-a-Good-Friend Composite

The two measures were highly correlated (r = .74, CI [.69, .79], p < .001) and were therefore combined into one measure. The overall model was significant (F(5, 295) = 5.56, p < .001), with R²adj = .07. There was a significant main effect of agent (F(2, 295) = 4.09, p = .018, p2 = .03), a main effect of action (F(1, 295) = 4.18, p = .042, p2 = .01), as well as an agent × action interaction (F(2, 295) = 7.79, p = .001, p2 = .05). Covert: Loyalty Composite Results Note. Covert, loyalty/friend (LF) composite as a function of agent × action. Error bars are SEs; ns are the same as listed above under positivity.

As depicted in Figure 7, the patterns coincide with those for positivity. However, retaliation (M = 5.90, SD = 1.71, CI [5.62, 6.18]) was favored over overall non-retaliation, though the difference did not reach significance (M = 5.54, SD = 1.65, CI [5.27, 5.80]; t(299) = 1.87, p = .062, d = 0.22, CI [-0.01, 0.44]). Participants regarded retaliating on behalf of a friend (M = 6.35, SD = 2.08, CI [5.74, 6.96]) as more indicative of being loyal and being a good friend than retaliating for both oneself (M = 5.69, SD = 1.24, CI [5.34, 6.04]; t(95) = 1.92, p = .059, d = 0.39, CI [-0.01, 0.79]) and third party (M = 5.68, SD = 1.70, CI [5.20, 6.16]), t(95) = 1.75, p = .084, d = 0.36, CI [-0.05, 0.76], but in each case the comparisons were not significant. The selfand third-party retaliation did not differ, t(98) = 0.03, p = .973, d = 0.00, CI [-0.40, 0.39].

In contrast, abstaining from retaliation for oneself was judged to be more globally loyal (M = 6.31, SD = 1.42, CI [5.92, 6.70]) than for a friend (M = 5.22, SD = 1.57, CI [4.78, 5.66]; t(101) = 3.71, p < .001, d = 0.73, CI [0.33, 1.13]) or for a third party (M = 5.04, SD = 1.67, CI [4.57, 5.51]); t(102) = 4.18, p < .001, d = 0.82, CI [0.42, 1.22]). The latter two conditions did not differ: t(99) = 0.56, p = .576, d = 0.11, CI [-0.28, 0.50]. Moreover, choosing not to retaliate for oneself was rated as more suggestive of loyalty than retaliating for oneself (t(101) = 2.36, p = .020, d = 0.46, CI [0.07, 0.86]).

Covert Enhanced Model: Adding Loyalty as Predictor and Moderator

Finally, adding the loyalty composite (mean-centered) as a predictor of positivity (Table 7) revealed a strong main effect (b = 0.73, SE = 0.05; F(1, 289) = 214.94, p < .001, p2 = .43).

While the main, compact model (agent × action) had an R² adj of .05, the R² adj for the enhanced model was much higher at .45; comparison of the two models produced F(6, 289) = 37.42, p < .001, with ΔAIC = 161.03, ΔBIC = 138.78. Further, adding loyalty rendered the main effect of agent non-significant. Loyalty did not reliably interact with other predictors; the closest effect was the three-way interaction which was not quite significant (F(2, 289) = 2.91, p = .056, p2 = .02). The agent × action remained significant at F(2, 289) = 3.10, p = .047, p2 = .02. Such results indicate that for covert contexts in which condition differences are weaker, evaluations still may be strongly structured by perceived loyalty. (Mediation results are provided in Supplemental Materials.)

Further Analysis

Comparing the Effect of Transparency ("Overt" vs. "Covert") Across Scenarios

Figure 8 depicts overt and covert positivity results side by side. Because we expected and observed distinctly different patterns within the two scenarios, we combined them in a mixed regression. This allowed us to examine how transparency (overt vs. covert retaliation) operates across contexts that differ markedly in proportionality-that is, in the perceived appropriateness of retaliation relative to the offense. In addition to other aspects of proportionality that differ between the two, transparency is perhaps most salient: It may be an essential dimension of proportionality, insofar as actions must be visible in order to ( 1) exert a strong impact on bystanders and (2) undergo social evaluation by those affected, directly or indirectly. Overt vs. Covert Positivity Note. Study 2a vs. Study 2b: Comparison of praiseworthiness/goodness of action across two vignettes, (A) overt, "Open-confrontation" vignette; original (typo-affected) self values were used for mixed analyses (and comparisons) because they were tied to participant identity, and (B) Covert, "Office-promotion" vignette, each as a function of agent identity (self, friend, third party) and action (retaliation, no retaliation). Error bars represent SEs of the means. Overarching bracket in (A) indicates a main effect of action.

Methods: Overt vs. Covert Scenarios

We conducted a mixed-effects ANOVA with transparency (overt vs. covert) as a withinsubjects factor, and agent (self vs. friend vs. third party) and action (retaliation vs. no retaliation) as between-subjects factors. Order of vignette presentation was included as a covariate and tested as a possible moderator of transparency, though we removed this interaction once it was clear that it was not significant and did not improve the fit of the model. This analysis thus constituted a 3 × 2 × 2 factorial design, estimated in a mixed-effects framework with random intercepts for participant.foot_1

Results: Mixed Model

The overall model explained a considerable amount of variance (R²m = 0.30; R²c = 0.49).

Controlling for order, the three-way interaction (agent × action × transparency) was not significant (F(2, 555.78) = 1.95, p = .144, p2 = .01), nor the interaction between transparency and agent (F(2, 550.64) = 1.97, p = .140, p2 = .01). Of greater interest, there was a main effect of transparency (F(1, 297.94) = 8.91, p = .003, p2 = .03), along with a strong interaction between transparency and action (F(1, 534.29) = 99.53, p < .001, p2 = .16). When collapsed across all six conditions within each scenario, the covert scenario (M = 5.52, SD = 1.81, CI [5.32, 5.73]) was rated more positively than the overt scenario (M = 5.12, SD = 2.55, CI [4.84, 5.41]).

With regard to retaliation, both scenarios were rated positively with overt (M = 6.71, SD = 2.02, CI [6.38, 7.03]), exceeding covert (M = 5.65, SD = 1.70, CI [5.37, 5.92]), as reflected in the simple effect (F(1, 425.11) = 28.98, p < .001, p2 = .06). However, the non-retaliation conditions diverged sharply. Choosing not to retaliate in the covert scenario (M = 5.40, SD = 1.90, CI [5.10, 5.70]) was judged much more positively than abstaining in the overt scenario (M = 3.55, SD = 1.99, CI [3.23, 3.88]; F(1, 422.27) = 89.82, p < .001, p2 = .18). Thus, the scenarios did not show simple inverse patterns: The overt scenario was more polarized-retaliation, high; no retaliation, low-while the covert-scenario was more measured, possibly reflecting ambivalence. In addition, abstaining from retaliating for oneself was rated more highly in the covert scenario (M = 6.07, SD = 1.70, CI [5.61, 6.52]) than in the overt scenario (M = 4.12, SD = 2.17, CI [3.49, 4.75]; (F(1, 532.54) = 31.37, p < .001, p2 = .06). Interestingly, retaliation for oneself in overt (M = 5.99, SD = 1.98, CI [5.44, 6.54]) did not differ from retaliation for oneself in the covert scenario (M = 5.46, SD = 1.57, CI [5.02, 5.90]; (F(1, 519.09) = 1.34, p = .247, p2 = .00).

Including the loyalty composite (grand-mean centered) as an added predictor and moderator revealed a strong main effect of loyalty (b = 0.77, SE = 0.03; F(1, 601.94) = 519.27, p < .001, p2 = .46). The enhanced model provided a substantially better fit than the main model (F(12, 546.89) = 44.02, p < .001, with ΔAIC = 368.7, ΔBIC = 315.9). Further, the enhanced model accounted for considerably more variance (R²m = 0.64; R²c = 0.72) than did the main (R²m = 0.30; R²c = 0.49). Although the transparency × action interaction was attenuated, it remained robustly significant (F(1, 568.61) = 25.36, p < .001, p2 = .04). The enhanced model also revealed a significant agent × action × transparency interaction (F(2, 573.55) = 5.50, p = .004, p2 = .02). (Full results are provided in the Supplemental Materials.)

Study 2 Overall Discussion

In the overt scenario, retaliating for a friend was rated significantly more praiseworthy than the other two conditions. Likewise, failing to retaliate for oneself was judged less blameworthy than for friend or third party. These patterns are especially notable because people tend to value impartiality in many fairness contexts.

By contrast, the covert scenario reflected greater ambivalence toward retaliation. One explanation is that in overt contexts, retaliation signals integrity and prosocial commitment, whereas covert retaliation lacks this visibility and imperative of proportionality-i.e., being warranted or normatively sanctioned. In that context, abstaining from retaliation for oneself was rated more positively than all other five conditions, reinforcing the findings that retaliation and non-retaliation do not always mirror one another. People evaluate them differently and may regard not retaliating as an active decision, requiring agency.

Notably, the loyalty ratings aligned with the praiseworthiness ratings in both studies.

These results provide support for retaliation as the particular domain of allies-and the corollary possibility that retaliation is a partial mechanism. When approved, people seem to regard retaliating for a friend as a positive form of partiality-loyalty-rather than a negative onefavoritism. Further, these characteristics suggest retaliation differs from other examples of assistance if it qualifies as aid at all.

STUDY 3: RETALIATION VS. HELP

Study 3 examined this possibility by directly comparing an act of retaliation and one of help, focusing only on friend and third-party comparisons. We predicted that in the helping context, assisting a stranger would be rated as more praiseworthy than helping a friend: As reviewed, people are regarded as having obligations to friends; they may, therefore, believe helping a stranger is more commendable because it goes "beyond" duty to close others.

By contrast, in our studies participants consistently rated retaliating for a friend as more commendable than for a stranger, sometimes significantly but always in magnitude. We, therefore, predicted opposing patterns: Retaliation would be rated more positively for friends than strangers, whereas help would be rated more positively for strangers than friends. This contrast provided an additional test of our more speculative second hypothesis: that retaliation is essentially partial, perhaps the particular mandate of allies.

Methods

Methods 200 participants (M(age) = 39.3, SD = 11.47) were recruited through Turk Prime for the study, completing the experiment in a median time of 3.38 minutes; 89 were female.

Participants

They were compensated $0.40. None were excluded from analyses.

Design and Procedure

We employed a 2 (Agent: friend vs. third party) × 2 (Action: Retaliation vs. Helping) betweensubjects design. Materials included the basic vignette from Study 2 and an additional "assistance" scenario in which the agent gives his after-hours office time to help a co-worker (friend or unknown, third party), who is struggling with a project. Participants read one of the following vignettes:

Table 8 Study 3-Wording for the "Retaliation" and "Help" Conditions Set-up Text Set-up for retaliation:

Set-up for help:

FRIEND: Jordan and Alex are long-time co-workers at a company, who have become good friends over the years and mutually relied upon one another's help and support. They also work with Casey, though neither of them knows him very well. The three have recently been assigned to jointly lead a very lucrative project for an important client. The project involves a large team and much creative and arduous work. Technically the most senior of the three, Jordan has the authority to oversee the team and remove people at his discretion.

THIRD PARTY: Jordan, Alex, and Casey are long-time co-workers at a company; they don't know each other very well but have recently been assigned to jointly lead a very lucrative project for an important client. The project involves a large team and much creative and arduous work. Technically the most senior of the three, Jordan has the authority to oversee the team and remove people at his discretion. At events and meetings, Jordan has observed that Casey has a habit of targeting Alex for ridicule and generally being unnecessarily rough and difficult with him.

FRIEND: Jordan and Alex are long-time co-workers at a company, who have become good friends over the years and mutually relied upon one another's support. Recently, each has been assigned to separate long-term projects involving important clients. They have both been under a lot of pressure and working long hours.

THIRD PARTY: Jordan and Alex are long-time co-workers at a company, who don't know each other well and have never exchanged more than a few words. Recently, each has been assigned to separate long-term projects involving important clients. They have both been under a lot of pressure and working long hours.

Retaliation: (friend and third At an early team meeting, Casey shocks Jordan by taking credit for a lot of Alex's work, mocking Alex each time he tries to state an party):

Helping behavior: (friend and third party) opinion, and basically dictating the next steps he thinks Alex should be taking in the project.

Jordan is mortified on Alex's behalf and knows Casey's behavior to be wrong. In front of the whole group, he sternly reprimands Casey and throws him off the project.

One night, Alex is overwhelmed with work involving one particular subject for which he does not have a lot of familiarity; he has hit a serious snag and is struggling to get a document completed before an impending deadline.

Jordan happens to run into him as the two are breaking for coffee, and when Alex vents about his stress, Jordan decides to put aside his own work for the night and talk Alex through the major issues holding him up. While he has no stake whatsoever in Alex's project, Jordan knows that he has far more expertise in such areas. With his help, Alex will be able to complete the document much more efficiently and effectively.

Note.

Agents acting as retaliators appear in red or as "helpers" in purple; friends appear in blue, third parties in green. Retaliation vignettes are almost identical to those used in Study 2a, except that the names and a few minor details have been changed.

Participants then provided the same evaluative ratings as in previous studiespraiseworthiness/blameworthiness, goodness/badness, loyalty, and friend ratings-each registered on bipolar 9-point scales. In addition, Study 3 included two new behavioral measures.

The first asked participants to appraise how likely the agent would be to help in the future. The second asked how likely the agent would be to retaliate in future. The exact wording appears in Figure 9. These questions were included to gauge what people infer about an agent's tendencies toward help or retaliation when exposed to an instance of one or the other.

retaliator (M = 6.83, SD = 2.38, CI [6.14, 7.51]), though only in magnitude (t(85.95) = 1.26, p = .210, d = 0.25, CI [-0.14, 0.65]). Moreover, the behavior of the third-party helper (M = 8.43, SD = 1.04, CI [8.13, 8.73]) was not significantly greater than that of the corresponding friend (M = 8.21, SD = 1.14, CI [7.89, 8.53]; t(98.85) = 1.00, p = .319, d = 0.20, CI [-0.19, 0.59]).

Enhanced Model.

Including loyalty as an additional predictor substantially increased explained variance in positivity (F(7, 192) = 57.57, p < .001, with R² adj = .67 and ΔR 2 adj = 0.54) but did not reveal significant interactions with agent or behavior. The main effect of composite loyalty (centered) was strong (b = 0.86, SE = 0.06; F(1, 192) = 222.83, p < .001, p2 = .54), and notably, the model also extracted a main effect of agent (F(1, 192) = 6.15, p = .014, p2 = .03).

(See Table 9.) Moreover, adding loyalty (enhanced model) significantly improved the main model's explanatory power when the two were compared directly (F(4, 192) = 80.50, p < .001).

Further, ΔAIC = 188.95 and ΔBIC = 175.76, respectively.

Loyalty/Likelihood-of-Being-a-Good-Friend Composite

Finding loyalty and the likelihood of being a good friend to be sufficiently correlated (r = 0.79, p < .001), we combined the measures. The overall model was significant: F(3, 196) = 13.97, p < .001, with R² adj = .16. Because the homogeneity-of-variance assumption was violated (Levene's test: F(3, 196) = 11.06, p < .001), planned comparisons were conducted using Welchadjusted tests.

The model yielded a significant main effect of agent (F(1, 196) = 8.56, p = .004, p2 = .04) and notably, a strong main effect of behavior (F(1, 196) = 26.85, p < .001, p2 = .12), with the help conditions exceeding overall retaliation. There was also an interaction between agent and behavior (F(1, 196) = 6.86, p = .009, p2 = .03). The agent retaliating on behalf of a friend (M = 7.71, SD = 1.33, CI [7.33, 8.09]) was deemed more loyal and more likely to be a good friend than the agent retaliating on behalf of an acquaintance (M = 6.52, SD = 2.30, CI [5.86, 7.18]; t(76.75) = 3.14, p = .002, d = 0.63, CI [0.22, 1.04]). Evaluations of the agent helping the friend (M = 8.26, SD = 1.08, CI [7.96, 8.56]) did not differ from those of the agent helping a third-party acquaintance (M = 8.19, SD = 1.03, CI [7.90, 8.49]; t(98.96) = 0.31, p = .755, d = 0.06, CI [-0.33, 0.45]).

Inferred Tendency to Help Others in Future

The overall model was significant: F(3, 196) = 6.06, p < .001, with R² adj = .07.

There was a main effect of behavior (F(1, 196) = 13.18, p < .001, p2 = .06), with the help conditions rated more likely to "help someone in need" than the retaliation conditions. The main effect of agent was not significant (F(1, 196) = 0.15, p = .696, p2 = .00), though there was a significant interaction between agent and behavior: F(1, 196) = 5.17, p = .024, p2 = .03. The model failed the assumption of variance homogeneity (Levene's test: F(3, 196) = 6.19, p < .001), and we thus proceeded with Welch comparisons.

The third-party helper (M = 8.41, SD = 0.79, CI [8.18, 8.63]) was judged more characteristically helpful than the corresponding agent acting for a friend (M = 7.85, SD = 1.30, CI [7.48, 8.21]; t(84.69) = 2.64, p = .010, d = 0.52, CI [0.12, 0.92]). The retaliation conditions did not differ, though the actions of the agent retaliating for a friend (M = 7.56, SD = 1.49, CI [7.14, 7.98]) were rated in magnitude as more reflective of general helpfulness than those of the agent acting for a third party (M = 7.16, SD = 2.10, CI [6.56, 7.77]); t(86.45) = 1.08, p = .281, d = 0.22, CI [-0.18, 0.61]).

Inferred Likelihood to Retaliate for Oneself

The overall model was significant with F(3, 196) = 15.8, p < .001 and R² adj = .18.

Levene's test upheld the assumption of homogeneity of variance (F(3, 196) = 1.24, p = .295), and we therefore proceeded with independent-samples comparisons.

The model failed to produce a main effect of agent (F(1, 196) = 1.79, p = .183, p2 = .01), though the main effect of behavior was strongly significant (F(1, 196) = 45.57, p < .001, p2 = .19), with overall retaliation exceeding overall help. However, ratings for both were modest, with only retaliation narrowly above the midpoint. In addition, the interaction between agent and behavior was not significant (F(1, 196) = 0.12, p = .727, p2 = .00). In both cases-retaliation and help-the friend was judged to be more inclined to retaliate for himself, though neither comparison was significant: neither friend (M = 5.80, SD = 2.15, CI [5.19, 6.41]) vs. third-party retaliator (M = 5.33, SD = 2.07, CI [4.73, 5.92]; t(97) = 1.12, p = .266, d = 0.22, CI [-0.17, 0.62]), nor friend (M = 3.81, SD = 1.82, CI [3.30, 4.31]) vs. third-party helper (M = 3.53, SD = 1.89, CI [2.99, 4.07]); t(99) = 0.75, p = .455, d = 0.15, CI [-0.24, 0.54]).

Study 3 Discussion

Although positivity results were not significant, they suggested the predicted pattern, with retaliation for a friend rated higher than retaliation for a third party and vice versa for the "help" conditions. The help conditions, especially, were also close to ceiling, which may have obscured the effects.

Inferred loyalty has consistently differentiated retaliatory conditions from one anothersignaling willingness to defend close others over strangers-whereas extending aid to strangers was indicative of future helpfulness, reinforcing separable processes. The measures revealed additional asymmetries: Perceptions of retaliation coincided with expectations of future helpfulness (i.e., outside primary domain), but helping did not suggest a tendency to retaliate.

These patterns support our broader claim that retaliation is the particular purview of allies and reflect mechanisms and heuristics qualitatively distinct from both assistance and third-party enforcement.

STUDY 4: RETALIATION VS. HELPING-GOSSIP

Study 4 had two primary objectives: first, to examine another case of helping behavior and assess whether results would replicate the pattern observed in Study 3; second, to introduce an office scenario involving a mode of retaliation we expected participants to condemn. We predicted that people might no longer favor retaliation for allies and might even rate third-party retaliation higher, as a disreputable act could undermine any obligation to engage in it. Third parties could at least appear less self-interested. If considered vindictive, the act might also shift appraisals of loyalty from high-minded commitment to mean-spirited favoritism.

Methods

Methods 194 participants (M(age) = 40.8, SD = 10.5) were recruited through Turk Prime for the study, completing the experiment in a median time of 6.16 minutes; 80 were female.

Participants

They were compensated $0.40. None were excluded from analyses.

Design and Procedure

As noted, we employed a 2 (Agent: friend vs. third party) × 2 (Action: Retaliation vs. Helping) between-subjects design, using what we expected would be considered an egregious act of retaliation and an additional "assistance" scenario in which the agent devotes his after-hours office time to help either a friend or an unknown co-worker with bringing a large load of files upstairs from the lobby. Participants read one of the following vignettes:

Table 10 Study 4-Wording for the "Retaliation" and "Help" conditions, respectively Set-up Text Set-up for retaliation (friend and third party):

FRIEND: Taylor and Jordan are long-time employees in the marketing department of Company X who have become good friends over the years.

Derek also works in the department, though neither knows him well and have never really exchanged more than a few words in passing. Recently, all three have been assigned to a large team for a potentially lucrative project attached to an important client. In the course of completing the project, the team has had to collaborate and discuss details over many meetings. Before long, it becomes clear that Derek has a tendency to single Jordan out for bad feedback and ridicule; in meetings, he constantly interrupts Jordan, dismisses his ideas, and ends up sticking him with the most menial of tasks..

THIRD PARTY: Taylor, Jordan, and

Derek are long-time employees in the marketing department of Company X. They don't know each other very well and have never really exchanged more than a few words in passing. Recently, all three have been assigned to a large team for a potentially lucrative project attached to an important client. In the course of completing the project, the team has had to collaborate and discuss details over many meetings. Before long, it becomes clear that Derek has a tendency to single Jordan out for bad feedback and ridicule; in meetings, he constantly interrupts Jordan, dismisses his ideas, and ends up sticking him with the most menial of tasks.

this project, Taylor spends a 1/2 hour or so helping Jordan to get the materials in manageable order.

Note. Agents acting as retaliators appear in red or as helpers in purple; friends appear in blue, third parties in green; retaliation offender printed in bold font

After randomization into one of the four conditions, participants completed exactly the same procedure as in the previous study. They first answered counterbalanced questions regarding praiseworthiness, goodness of action, propensity to be loyal, and likelihood of being a good friend. Once again, the four measures were found to be highly correlated and combined into two. Participants then completed the same two counterbalanced behavioral questions from Study 3, one targeted at inferences regarding prosociality and the other at deterrence-or exhibition of strong character, unlikely to tolerate negative behavior towards themselves.

Results

Positivity: Praiseworthiness/Goodness of Action Composite

Finding the praiseworthiness and goodness of action measures to be highly correlated (r = 0.91, p < .001), we combined them into a single measure of positivity. As exhibited in Table 11, the model was significant (F(3, 190)= 146.5, p < .001), with R² adj = .69. The main effect of agent and the interaction were negligible. Consistent with the prediction that retaliating would be judged severely, there was a very strong main effect of behavior (F(1, 190)= 439.51, p < .001, p2 = .70). The model failed the assumption of homogeneity of variance (Levene's test: F(3, 190) = 8.10, p < .001), and we therefore used Welch comparisons.

As expected, people rated retaliation very negatively for the first time, with retaliation for friend (M = 3.38, SD = 1.81, CI [2.86, 3.90]) and retaliation for third party (M = 3.37, SD = 2.00, CI [2.80, 3.94]) about dead even. The helping means clustered towards the top of the scale, with helping a third party (M = 8.03, SD = 0.93, CI [7.76, 8.30]) no higher than aiding a friend (M = 8.06, SD = 1.18, CI [7.72, 8.40]). Adding loyalty (centered) yielded a strong main effect (B = .72, SE = .07; F(1, 186) = 116.63, p < .001, p2 = .39), a main effect of behavior (F(1, 186) = 59.11, p < .001, p2 = .24), and a main effect of agent (F(1, 186) = 4.14, p = .043, p2 = .02), though there were no interactions.

Enhanced Model. Once again, adding loyalty as a predictor and moderator considerably improved model fit, explaining more variance than the compact (F(7, 186) = 133.7, p < .001, with R² adj = .83 and ΔR 2 adj = 0.13). The main effect of loyalty (centered) was robust (b = 0.72, SE = 0.07; F(1, 192) = 116.53, p < .001, p2 = .39), and controlling for loyalty revealed a significant main effect of agent (F(1, 192) = 4.14 p =.043, p2 = .02). The main effect of behavior remained robustly significant (F(1, 192) = 59.12, p < .001, p2 = .24). Directly compared, the enhanced model improved upon the main model in terms of explanatory power (F(4, 186) = 38.14, p < .001) and fit (ΔAIC = 108.19 and ΔBIC = 95.11).

Loyalty/Likelihood-of-Being-a-Good-Friend Composite

With the measures highly correlated (r = 0.81, p < .001), we combined general likeliness of being loyal and being a good friend into one DV. As exhibited in Table 11, the model was significant (F(3, 190) = 87.87, p < .001), with an R² adj = .57. Despite a main effect of agent (F(1, 190) = 8.56, p = .004, p2 = .04) and a large main effect of behavior (F(1, 190) = 252.74, p < .001, p2 = .57), there was no interaction (F(1, 190) = 1.19, p = .278, p2 = .01). The model failed the assumption of homogeneity (Levene's test: F(3, 190) = 9.36, p < .001), so we used Welch comparisons.

Though judged equally blameworthy, the loyalty evaluation of the retaliator acting for a friend was rated at (M = 4.89, SD = 1.76, CI = [4.38, 5.39]), which was significantly higher than its third-party counterpart (M = 4.03, SD = 1.88, CI = [3.50, 4.56]; t(96.82) = 2.34, p = .021, d = 0.47, CI [0.07, 0.87]). Likewise, the friend who helped (M = 8.05, SD = 1.03, CI [7.75, 8.35]) was rated higher in magnitude than the third-party helper but not significantly (M = 7.66, SD = 1.03, CI [7.36, 7.96]; t(92.94) = 1.86, p = .067, d = 0.38, CI [-0.03, 0.79]).

Inferred Tendency to Help

The overall model was significant (F(3, 190) = 28.52, p < .001), with an R² adj = .30. The main effect of agent was not significant (F(1, 190) = .05, p = .821, p2 = .00), while there was a strong main effect of behavior (F(1, 190) = 85.52, p < .001, p2 = .31). The interaction was not significant (F(1, 190) = .01, p = .906, p2 = .00). The model satisfied the assumption of homogeneity of variance (Levene's test: F(3, 190) = 2.20, p = .090), so we used independentsamples tests for the planned comparisons.

As expected, people inferred that those who engaged in helping behavior would be more likely to do so in future than either of the retaliators. There was no difference between third-party (M = 7.96, SD = 1.49, CI [7.52, 8.39]) and friend helping conditions (M = 7.88, SD = 1.30, CI [7.50, 8.25]; t(93) = 0.29, p = .774, d = 0.06, CI [-0.34, 0.46]). Despite the extremely blameworthy nature of both retaliators' actions, people seemed to infer that they would hypothetically assist others, or at least they were both modestly on the prosocial side of the scale.

We, therefore, tested if they were each statistically above the midpoint: friend (M = 5.69, SD = 1.73, CI [5.20, 6.19]; t(48) = 2.80, p = .007) and third party (M = 5.72, SD = 2.02, C1=[5.15, 6.29]; t(49) = 2.52, p = .015). As will be further demonstrated below, people apparently expect that even extreme retaliators are likely to help others, though not the inverse

Inferred Tendency to Retaliate for Oneself

The overall model was significant (F(3, 190) = 59.03, p < .001), with an R² adj = .47.

As with the other DVs, there was a strong effect of behavior (F(1, 190) = 176.69, p <.001, p2 = .48) but no effect of agent (F(1, 190) = 0.36, p = .549, p2 = .00) and no interaction between agent and behavior (F(1, 190) = 0.18, p = .669, p2 = .00). We were especially interested in how people would respond to retaliation for another-friend or third party-when the act was regarded as repugnant or disproportionate. The model satisfied the assumption of homogeneity of variance (Levene's test: F(3, 190) = 0.98, p = .403), so we used independent-samples tests for the planned comparisons.

Despite clearly finding the manner of retaliation to be blameworthy, people rated both retaliators-for friend (M = 7.47, SD = 1.56, CI [7.02, 7.92]) and third party (M = 7.20, SD = 1.99, CI [6.63, 7.77]) highly on this measure, indicating that retaliation did transmit to onlookers a proclivity for retaliating for oneself-a kind of deterrence signal. However, the two did not differ. As in Study 3, people did not appear to infer retaliatory characteristics from helping behavior, nor did they differ from one another: neither helping a friend (M = 3.88, SD = 1.93, CI [3.31, 4.44]), nor helping a third party (M = 3.83, SD = 1.79, CI [3.31, 4.35]).

Study 4 Discussion

The data provided evidence for our primary prediction for Study 4: People rated retaliation negatively and no longer favored retaliation for friends. Interestingly, loyalty was still rated higher for the friend condition, though both were low. People inferred both helping behavior from even this negative retaliatory action and a strong tendency to retaliate on their own behalf. This mode of retaliation-though condemned-evidently signaled that these agents were not to be trifled with, or in other words, exhibited a strong deterrence effect.

General Discussion

The study findings provide strong evidence that people factor in both the identity of the retaliator and, more specifically, the relationship between retaliator and victim when evaluating decisions to retaliate-or not. This evidence suggests retaliation is volatile and subject to circumstances outside the original provocation-and a valuable resource people believe must be used judiciously. Further, the respective patterns in the retaliation and non-retaliation contexts were not simple inversions of one another. In particular, both retaliating for allies and choosing not to retaliate for oneself operate as delicate reputational signals-especially as overall retaliation shifts from favorable or warranted to ambivalent.

To our knowledge, this is the first paper to directly compare evaluations of the actions of three central identities as they chose to engage in retaliation or abstain-especially as concerns valence, transparency, and loyalty, which proved a meaningful factor in our understanding of mechanism. It also contributes to a relative gap in studies exploring decisions about whether or not to engage in retaliation in real-life conditions. While a rising body of developing researchespecially involving economic games and third parties-has examined participant appraisals of retaliators, they have not often directly compared these three retaliator identities, especially in connection with overall approval of the retaliating act.

As represented most clearly by Study 2, judgments of overall retaliation coincided with different patterns among the identities we examined and their respective decisions. For instance, in Study 2a, failure to retaliate for a friend was lowest, but this time (unlike in Study 1) failure to retaliate for a third party was rated nearly as blameworthy and more blameworthy in Study 2b.

Study 2b suggested that the less retaliation was endorsed, different patterns asserted themselves /became apparent. These findings seemed to support the Integrity Hypothesis, part of which postulates that choosing not to retaliate for oneself (and for a friend) might represent a demonstration of magnanimity and/or transmit a message that this person has no need for retaliation. For instance, imagine an example in which someone has overheard a co-worker making her the butt of a joke. Retaliating might make her appear petty, whereas laughing it off may exhibit strength and self-assurance. Likewise, choosing to retaliate and to refrain on behalf of a friend-both regarded relatively highly in Study 2b-could reflect tensions between loyalty and favoritism. However, retaliating for an egregious wrong or even retaliating harshly also may be considered necessary-demonstrating strength-essentially "standing up" for oneself or others, as Study 4 suggested.

As Study 4 starkly exhibits, the mode and reception of retaliation may depend on the motivation-whether conscious or not-of the retaliator. While people clearly condemned the behavior, they nonetheless registered a deterrence effect. Both friend and third party were deemed likely to retaliate for a future offense and were also judged likely to be helpful-a combination that differs from the "helping" condition results, as do the patterns in Study 3. In some circumstances, retaliation may serve a purpose when disproportionately severe from an outsider's-and even a retaliator's-perspective. However, such gains may be short-term and also run the risk of alienating people who might regard the retaliation as excessive or frightening.

Future Directions

Conversely, it's likely that third parties are supported for their supposed impartiality in related decision-making but not as much with regard to retaliation for an offense among relative peers. Future areas of investigation could include manipulating whether the various retaliators are in positions of authority-in which case, a third-party might be vested with a different power: that of being an institutional third-party rather than just a bystander or peer (e.g., Kim et al., 1998). Third parties may acquire distinction when invested with the proper status. "Retaliation" may then seem more their jurisdiction and, therefore, more praiseworthy. Indeed, much evidence has indicated that third parties-especially on an institutional level-have doubtlessly contributed to the maintaining of order in small ancient groups, as well as modern times (e.g., Boehm, 2019).

However, as perhaps our most tantalizing result, our studies demonstrate that such characteristics-impartiality in particular-may not always translate into praiseworthiness or endorsement for retaliation. We argue that retaliation is fundamentally a partial mechanism, one that appears to be the purview of allies. Afterall, when warranted, retaliation for friends prevailed as most praiseworthy. Further, loyalty is a significant component in the mechanism underlying evaluations of retaliating-moderating and partially mediating the relationship between condition and praiseworthiness. Consistent across studies, loyalty as a DV also tended to track praiseworthiness-and to distinguish among identities, even when praiseworthiness did not. One possible reason is that people regard retaliation-even when likely to be condoned-as inherently costly; it therefore transmits a strong, relational signal of loyalty. Indeed, retaliation for allies may thus constitute a separate mechanism from third-party retaliation, which likely has its own parameters-especially given that impartial third parties or institutions have not always been available or willing to intervene. Such a possibility would coincide with Shinada & colleagues' 2004 results demonstrating that people are more intent on punishing members of their ingroup-vs. outgroup-presumably to provide order within their own community. Our results provide some insight into this possible separate nature.

Undoubtedly, further research should how retaliation for allies and strangers may fundamentally differ.

Overarchingly, we set out to explore how identities and relationships affect how people evaluate retaliation. Such a pursuit is significant because it is precisely onlookers-in addition to offenders-who stand to judge retaliation and those who engage in it by conferring benefits or administering costs. In investigating retaliation-or the decision not to-for self, friend, and third party, we found that it can vary widely. Yet distinct patterns emerged the more people endorsed or censured it. Our findings have built on past research by suggesting that retaliation is a partial, valuable mechanism-likely the domain of allies-and is distinct from both third-party norm enforcement and straightforward assistance.

Transparency and Openness

The initial studies were designed and conducted prior to the widespread adoption of preregistration practices. For consistency, we chose not to preregister the later studies in this series. Nonetheless, we clearly distinguish a priori hypotheses from exploratory analyses, and we outline the development of our predictions across studies. All data, materials, and analysis code will be made available and can be provided upon request. All Measures in Study 1: Praiseworthiness, Loyalty, Altruism, Morality, and Heroism Note. Mediation

Study 1 Mediation

As displayed in Figure S2, we used the agent × action structure to directly examine whether perceptions of loyalty mediated evaluations of praiseworthiness. All three mediation analyses used bootstrapping (5,000 draws) and sum-to-zero contrasts to provide estimated a-, b- Consistent with our praiseworthiness and loyalty models, action was partially mediated by loyalty (ab = 0.85, SE = 0.09, CI [0.67, 1.04], z = 9.06, p < .001), while indirect effects for agent (ab = 0.08, SE = 0.08, CI [-0.08, 0.23], z = 0.95, p = .343) and the agent × action interaction (ab = -0.04, SE = 0.08, CI [-0.21, 0.12], z = -0.54, p = .593) were not significant. Controlling for loyalty, direct effects were not significant for agent (c′ = -0.17, SE = 0.11, CI [-0.40, 0.05], z = -1.48, p = .139) or the interaction (c′ = 0.04, SE = 0.12, CI [-0.18, 0.27], z = .38, p = .706), whereas action remained significant (c′ = 0.45, SE = 0.11, CI [0.23, 0.68], z = 3.95, p < .001).

Such results support findings indicating that while loyalty reliably tracks praiseworthiness to some degree, the two measurements do not always neatly coincide. People may have regarded restoring stolen property, along with harsh words for the thief, as overwhelmingly praiseworthy and, partially for that reason, evaluations may have failed to differentiate among the identities on the retaliation side. However, loyalty evaluations did significantly differ among the identities on the retaliation side, with friend highest, followed by third party-recall that self and third party differed in magnitude but not significance.

Interestingly, differences among the agents on the non-retaliation side largely coincided with the predictions of the Integrity Hypothesis: Failing a friend was most blameworthy and disloyal and abstaining on one's own behalf least blameworthy and most loyal. Perhaps most notably, the coded to sum to zero, and using bootstrapped standard errors (5,000 draws). Unsurprisingly,

Figure S3

Overt Mediation of Loyalty Between Condition and Positivity Using Agent × Action Structure

We predicted that, in the case of retaliation, the friend condition would be rated higher than the third-party condition. In the help cases, we expected the opposite configuration, with helping a stranger rated higher than helping a friend. In further contrast, we expected that this act would be reflected in perception of increased future helpfulness. In addition, we anticipated that helping would be construed as a signal of future helpfulness, whereas retaliation would signal a broader tendency to retaliate.

Positivity: Praiseworthiness/Goodness-of-Action Composite

Finding the praiseworthiness and goodness-of-action measures to be highly correlated (r = .85, p < .001), we combined them into a single "positivity" measure. As depicted in Table 9, the overall model was significant: F(3, 196) = 10.29, p < .001, with R² adj = .12. Contrary to our prediction, the main effect of agent (F(1, 196) = 0.44, p = .510, p2 = .00) was not significant, nor was the interaction between agent and behavior (F(1, 196) = 2.55, p = .112, p2 = .01), though the pattern was in the predicted direction. However, there was a strong main effect of behavior (F(1, 196) = 28.17, p < .001, p2 = .13), with helping rated higher than retaliation.

Because the model failed the assumption of homogeneity of variance (Levene's test: F(3, 196) = 10.32, p < .001), we conducted Welch comparisons. The actions of the retaliator friend were rated more highly (M = 7.35, SD = 1.67, CI [6.87, 7.83]) than those of the third-party Set-up for help (friend and third party): FRIEND: Taylor and Jordan are long-time employees in the marketing department of Company X, who have become very good friends over the years. Recently, Jordan has been assigned to lead a potentially lucrative project for an important client. At the start of the project, Jordan orders all of the background files to be sent over on a Saturday and sets aside that day to receive them and start going through them.

THIRD PARTY: Taylor and Jordan are long-time employees in the marketing department of Company X. They don't know each other very well and have never really exchanged more than a few words in passing. Recently, Jordan has been assigned to lead a potentially lucrative project for an important client. At the start of the project, Jordan orders all of the background files to be sent over on a Saturday and sets aside that day to receive them and start going through them.

One day, Taylor is busy compiling a report from documents that he has received from various members of the team when he comes across a private email chain Derek has passed along accidentally. The chain contains very personal emails between Derek and his wife, suggesting that Derek is having an extramarital affair. That night, Taylor meets up with some co-workers for cocktails. When Derek's name comes up, he tells the group that he has it on good authority that Derek has been cheating on his wife.

That Saturday, the files are delivered in the lobby, and he discovers that the load is much bigger and in greater disarray than he has supposed. None of his team and few admin are around to assist as the building is mostly empty for the weekend.

Just as he is starting to load the first batch onto the elevator, he runs into Taylor, who had stopped by work to catch up on some of his own projects and is now headed home. Taylor sees what Jordan has on his hands and offers to help get the boxes upstairs. Though it is not at all his job to assist in this way and he has nothing to do with Supplemental Materials

Power Analysis

Sample sizes were determined based on prior work and standard practices in the field.

We aimed for approximately 50 participants per cell in each condition, which provides adequate power to detect the small-to-moderate effects typical of this literature.

Study 1 mediation reinforces that loyalty broadly distinguishes between those who retaliate and those who do not.

Study 2a: Overt Mediation

Mediation results demonstrate that the basic agent × action structure is partially mediated by perceptions of loyalty, consistent with the possibility that it is a fundamental part of the mechanism by which people construe and evaluate retaliation. Figure S3 provides the main coefficients for each portion of the mediation, with loyalty mean-centered, the a-path contrasts Note. Study 2b: Covert mediation

The covert mediation coincided with our regressions and added nuance to the previous mediations. Figure S4 , z = 2.49, p = .013) and also mediated action, though to lesser degree (ab = 0.14, SE = 0.07, CI [0.00, 0.27], z = 1.99, p = .046). There was no evidence that the agent × action interaction was mediated by loyalty (ab = -0.09, SE = 0.10, CI [-0.28, 0.09], z = -0.96, p = .338). Controlling for loyalty, direct effects were not significant for agent (c′ = 0.18, SE = 0.12, CI [-0.06, 0.40], z = 1.47, p = .141), nor action (c′ = -0.01, SE = 0.08, CI [-0.16, 0.14], z = -0.06, p = .951), whereas the interaction remained significant (c′ = -0.25, SE = 0.12, CI [-0.47, -0.02], z = -2.17, p = .030). Such results support the broader interpretation that when retaliation is regarded with greater ambivalence and negativity, loyalty evaluations exert a weaker influence on the differentiation of positivity judgments across identities and between retaliation and no retaliation than in the overt context.

Typographical Error

Because of a minor typographical error in the original wording of the self conditions of the overt scenario, we re-ran these conditions with corrected text. Results did not meaningfully differ between versions. We inserted the corrected vignettes for Study 2a, which was betweensubjects. We used the original versions for mixed analyses because they were tied to participant responses.

Enhanced Mixed Model (Includes Loyalty-LF_composite, Mean-centered).

As reviewed in the manuscript, adding loyalty as predictor and moderator significantly improved upon the overall fit of the compact mixed model (F(12, 546.89) = 44.02, p < .001), with ΔAIC = 368.7, ΔBIC = 315.9).

The main model yielded R²m = 0.30, and R²c = 0.49, while this enhanced model accounted for considerably more variance R²m = 0.64, and R²c = 0.72, constituting a ΔR²m = .34 and ΔR²c = .23, respectively. The pertinent effects and interactions from the main, compact model remained significant. Table S4 presents the full results for the enhanced model.

We use "overt" and "covert" purely descriptively to distinguish forthright from clandestine retaliatory actions in our stimuli. Although organizational research occasionally uses these terms to categorize revenge behaviors (e.g.,McIlduff & Coghlan, 2000), that literature examines different phenomena (e.g., sabotage, workplace status) and does not directly bear on our studies.

The original self conditions were used because participants were randomly assigned to them; consequently, mixedmodel descriptives differed slightly from those reported in the overt-only analyses.
